• OmniSwitch xxxx Series Hardware Users Guide
• Switch hardware components and basic switch hardware
• OmniSwitch AOS Switch Management Guide
• Describes basic attributes of the switch and basic switch administration tasks
• OmniSwitch AOS Network Configuration Guide
• Describes how to set up and monitor software features that will allow the switch to operate 
in a live network environment
• OmniSwitch AOS Advanced Routing Configuration Guide
• Describes how to set up and monitor advanced routing protocols for operation in a live network environment
• OmniSwitch CLI Reference Guide
• Comprehensive resource to all Command Line Interface (CLI) commands available on the OmniSwitch products
• OmniSwitch Transceivers Guide
• Provides specifications and compatibility information for the supported OmniSwitch SFP and XFP transceivers
for all OmniSwitch AOS 6 Release Products

<<<PAGE 59>>>
Troubleshooting methodology
• Three other approaches 
Follow the path
Based on the path that packets take through the 
network from source to destination
Spot the differences
compares network devices or processes that are 
operating correctly to devices or processes that 
are not operating
Move the problem
To physically move components and observe 
whether the problem moves with the components

run rescue
The Rescue Utility reformats the switch compact flash memory. 
All files in compact flash will be lost and replaced with the contents of the /usb/6900/* from the USB storage device. 
This will take at least 10 minutes.

CHANGE BOOT DIRECTORY FROM U-BOOT
Step by Step
Break the boot sequence when you see the above message

u-boot> setenv bootfile working/Uos.img
u-boot> saveenv
u-boot> run bootcmd
The switch will now boot from the directory specified after bootfile
keyword.
The below command can be used to check that the bootfile has 
been changed to working/Uos.img.
u-boot> printenv
The bootfile has been changed to working/Uos.img.
Note that any directory path can be defined. 
For example: u-boot> setenv bootfile vc_dir/Uos.img.

<<<PAGE 109>>>
ADVANCED TROUBLE-SHOOTING

<<<PAGE 110>>>
TROUBLESHOOTING THE SWITCH SYSTEM
• The most common causes for high CPU utilization: 
• An abnormal process 
• A process goes into an infinite loop. This is probably a software issue. 
• A process is doing extensive calculations. It is possible that the network is not well scaled. 
• AOS is under a DoS attack. 
• Abnormal traffic 
• Too many messages exchanged between AOS subsystems. 
Examples include extensive logging, MAC learning, malfunctioning bus, HW interrupts. 
• Too many frames or packets are trapped to CPU 
• Use the commands “top” and “ps” in the maintenance shell to find the process(es) 
consuming the most CPU and use knowledge of the network to determine whether or not 
the consumption is abnormal. 
• Each process presents a task running in CPU.

<<<PAGE 111>>>
TROUBLESHOOTING THE SWITCH SYSTEM

• “top” and “ps” commands (maintenance shell)
• Continuously monitor the tasks consuming CPU -Provide a view of process activity in real time
Usage: top [-b] [-nCOUNT] [-dSECONDS] [-m]
Usage: ps [-o COL1,COL2=HEADER] [-T]
Keys:
N/M/P/T: show CPU usage, sort by pid/mem/cpu/time
S: show memory
R: reverse sort
H: toggle threads, 1: toggle SMP
Q,^C: exit
Options:
-b
Batch mode
-n N
Exit after N iterations
-d N
Delay between updates
-m
Same as 's' key
Show list of processes
-o COL1,COL2=HEADER
Select columns for display
(supported arguments: user, group, comm, args, pid, ppid, pgid, etime, nice, rgroup, 
ruser, time, tty, vsz, sid, stat, rss) ps –a
-T
Show threads
* If you identify a process causing high CPU , please contact Alcatel-Lucent customer support to know the procedure.

1
Client 7 pings Client 5 on the same VLAN (subnet)
2
Client 7 determines that destination Client 5 IP 
is on the same subnet
3
Client 7 consults its ARP cache, encapsulates the IP packet
in an Ethernet frame and transmits the frame to Client 5 
Vlan 10
Vlan 10
4
If Client 7 does not have an entry for Client 5 in its ARP cache,
it will ARP for the Client 5 MAC address.
5
The intermediate switches flood the ARP request 
over the 802.1Q links.
6
Client 5 sends a unicast ARP reply back to Client 7
7
Switches forward the ARP reply unicast frame toward Client 7
8
Client 7 encapsulates the IP packet (ICMP Echo Request)
in a unicast frame and sends it to Client 5
9
Switches forward ICMP Echo Request unicast frame 
toward Host B.
Switches forward ICMP Echo Reply unicast frame toward Client 7 
Host A Receives ICMP Echo Reply Back from Client 5

Issues that could cause the communication to fail
Physical problems
•
General health of the switch or NI 
•
Physical link status might not be 
operational...
MAC address not learned on the 
port
ARP request not reaching the switch, which may be 
possible because:
•
The workstation is not sending an ARP request
•
The workstation is not able to understand the 
ARP request
•
ARP packet might have got corrupted
•
Duplicate IP addresses configured on the 
workstations in the same VLAN
ARP is not getting 
resolved in either of the 
two workstations

Troubleshooting Switched Ethernet Connectivity
•
Layer 2 Diagnostic Tools :ARP problems
1.
Make sure that the MAC address of device A and device B are learned on the right port and in correct VLAN
2.     To confirm the MAC address of the routing instance
3.     Verify that the gateway defined in device A points towards the correct IP address
4.      When device A ARPs for the gateway IP address on the switch, an associated ARP cache entry is created by the switch.
To confirm that the switch has learned the ARP entry of the device.
5.      Check that the device has resolved the ARP entry to the gateway IP address

• If the MAC addresses is already learned on the port and the ARP is not getting resolved

Note :
If the switch is replying back to the ARP , the ARP 
cache of the workstation should also show the 
correct ARP entry for the switch.
If not, then a sniffer should be placed between 
the switch and the Workstation.

<<<PAGE 129>>>
Troubleshooting Switched Ethernet Connectivity
Layer 2 Diagnostic Tools : ARP Troubleshooting – Silent Device
•
ARP at the time of the bootup, if needed:
•
Increase MAC Address aging time can also be increased
•
Add silent devices MAC address in the MAC address table as permanent
-> mac-learning {vlan vlan_id {port chassis/slot/port | linkagg agg_id}} static mac-address mac_address [bridging | filtering] 
(R8) 
-> mac-learning aging-time {seconds | default} 
Note :
If the ARP cache of the switch is not showing the correct ARP entries for the edge devices then you can clear the ARP table and then re-learn all the ARP entries
-> clear arp-cache 
But, clearing the ARP cache might cause a slight interruption in the network, if done at peak hours and,on the Core switch. (re invoke the process of ARP learning)

1.2.1. 
Problem N1 
Description of the Problem 
After reloading of OS6900-V48C8 the 10GIG Uplink to OS6560-P48Z16 stays DOWN. 
Uplink of OS6560-P48Z16 switches are connected to VC of 2 X OS6900-V48C8 switches via linkagg. 
If the OS6900-V48C8 VC is reloaded, 1 or both the link in the linkagg to OS6560-P48Z16 stays DOWN. 
Version Build 
8.8.56.R02/8.9.73.R01/8.9.106.R02 
1.2.2. 
Problem N2 
Description of the Problem 
VC-8 OS6560 running AOS 8.5.265.R02 and having UNP ports configure 
Some UNP users remain in 'In progress' State (for untagged traffic) and clients connected to these ports 
face reachability issues. 
Version Build 
8.5.265.R02

network, physical and logical topology diagrams

- Physical Topology: This should show all the network devices and how they are physically connected to 
each other. 
- Logical topology: This should show how everything is connected to each other. Protocols that are 
- Interconnections: It’s useful to have on the diagram that shows which interfaces of one network device 
are connected to the interface of another network device.

SWITCH LOGGING OUTPUT
• Event logging utility 
• Useful in maintaining and servicing the switch
• Switch events can be logged to
• Switch console
• Local text file
• Configurable default file size
• 1250 Kbytes – R8
• Multiple remote devices (syslog)
• 12 max – R8
• Loopback0 have to be configured

(in bytes)

SWITCH LOGGING SEVERITY LEVEL 
• Default severity level is info. The numeric equivalent for info is 6
• It is also possible to assign different severity levels to different switch applications (some 
of the events will be filtered out of the display)

VIRTUAL CHASSIS TROUBLESHOOTING
Reminder
• Virtual Chassis = Group of Switches 
which appears as a single router or bridge
• Single Point of management 
• Single Logical Switch
• Redundancy and resiliency supported 
across the switches
• No STP/VRRP between Access and Core
• Optimized bandwidth usage
• Upgrade via ISSU
• Minimize network impact
• No license needed
• Switches inter-connected via dedicated 
• or optional SFP+, QSFP ports
• Mesh or Ring topology

<<<PAGE 159>>>
VIRTUAL CHASSIS - AUTO VFL PORT
• Goal
• Automatically detect whether an auto VFL port can become VFL
• Dynamically assign VFL ID to auto VFL port which can become VFL
• Aggregate multiple auto VFL ports that can become VFL and are connected to the same remote 
chassis
* Auto VFL detection process will run only on auto VFL ports. Both ends of the link must be auto VFL ports for an 
auto VFL port to be able to become VFL.

<<<PAGE 161>>>
VIRTUAL CHASSIS TROUBLESHOOTING
Boot sequence
• Upon boot-up, a switch will read its local vcsetup.cfg file and attempt to connect to the 
other neighbor switches 
• Upon connection, the switches will exchange the parameters configured in their local 
vcsetup.cfg files 
• As a result of this exchange, they will discover the topology, elect a Master 
• Start periodic health checks over the VFL, and synchronize their configuration as defined 
within the vcboot.cfg configuration file 
• All Slaves, if they do not have a local copy of vcboot.cfg, or if their local copy does not 
match the copy found on the Master, will download the vcboot.cfg from the Master chassis 
and reboot using this copy of vcboot.cfg as its configuration file 
error mode  
The vcsetup.cfg file is corrupted or edited in such a way that it is 
unable to read a valid chassis identifier in the appropriate range.
A switch start up error mode will keep all of its front-panel user 
ports, including the virtual-fabric links member ports disabled. 
Inconsistent Invalid-Chassis-Id
The chassis is not operational in virtual chassis mode because no 
valid chassis identifier has been found in the configuration. 
Typically, this means that tTypically,etup.cfg file is corrupted, 
empty or contains an invalid (e.g. out of range) chassis identifier

traffic utilization

drops on an interface

changes and flooding

interface

rate of STP Topology Changes (TC)

address flapping messages

of a bridging loop

address flapping messages

<<<PAGE 172>>>
SPANNING TREE ADVANCED TROUBLESHOOTING
Quick way to exit from a transition loop
Even better, if possible, start by disabling 
ports that should be blocking
Easy way out of a 
bridging loop 
Disable manually every single port that is 
providing redundancy in the network
The effect of the bridging loop 
should stop immediately after 
it is broken.
Note:  If this port should have been blocking, you have probably found the link on which the failure appeared.

• 4. Get some STP configuration details:
• Make sure that all devices are running the same STP mode (1x1, FLAT, MSTP)
• Which switch is the root and backup root  and designated ports on each subsequent switch
(for each VLAN)
• Determine the root bridge and make sure that it's on the right switch 
(Aggregation or core)
• Which links have a non-default cost or priority?
• What is the location of blocking ports?

<<<PAGE 179>>>
SPANNING TREE TROUBLESHOOTING
• 5. Get some STP configuration details:
• Verify the correct mode, designated root ID, root 
port, and configurable timers. 
• Timers need to be consistent across a physical link 
running STP. 
• It is also useful to note the number of Topology 
changes and Topology age. 
• If topology changes are incrementing quickly, the 
devices participating in spanning tree cannot agree 
who is the root bridge. 
• This can be caused by dropped BPDUs, a bridge that tries 
to change its role, or a physical link going in and out of 
service.
-> show spantree vlan 2

<<<PAGE 180>>>
SPANNING TREE TROUBLESHOOTING
• 6. Determine the port state 
• Remember that in any LAN with physical path redundancy there must be at least one port in blocking status. 
• Knowledge of the ports which are usually in a blocking state, can be leveraged as a starting point for 
troubleshooting. Has their state changed ?
• If ports that should be in a blocking state are now forwarding, there are two likely causes: 
• A physical failure in a link that was previously forwarding. 
• BPDUs from the root are being dropped. If it appears that BPDUs are being dropped, troubleshoot this as if it were any 
other packet being dropped.

• 7. Constant MAC address relearning and flapping
• MAC address flapping is mostly caused by a layer 2 loop in the network 
(which are not detected by STP)

between two ports.

• 8. Specific information for MSTP
• All devices should use the same region name
• All VLANs within an MSTI must be tagged on all 
interswitch links otherwise MSTP becomes 
unpredictable
• All switches participating in the same region 
must have an identical MSTP configuration.

Designing STP to Avoid Trouble
Avoid Tuning STP 
Parameters
Decide which bridge will be root 
using Priority parameter. 
Think about additional features
Loop-guard 
• Prune VLANs that Are Not Used
• Avoid Having a Single VLAN Spanning the Entire Network
• Keep Traffic Off the Administrative VLAN
Detail configuration information are useful at 
troubleshooting time
-Know Where the Root Is
-Know Where Redundancy Is
Minimize the Number of Blocked Ports
For each VLAN, know which ports should be blocking 
in the stable network. Have a Network diagram that 
clearly shows each physical loop in the network and 
which blocked ports break the loops
Choose a powerful bridge in the middle 
of the network
(Then reduce the average distance from 
the clients to the servers and routers.)
Recommendation
A single blocking port 
transitioning to forwarding by 
error can meltdown a big part 
of the network
Avoid using VLAN 1 as default VLAN. 
Use different default Vlan .
A single blocking port transitioning 
to forwarding by error can 
meltdown a big part of the network
Take special care if you plan 
to change STP timers from 
their default values (impact 
on diameter and stability of 
the STP)
The only parameters that you 
may want to change are the 
bridge priority (to select the 
root bridge) and the port cost 
or priority (to control 
redundancy and load 
balancing)
qos user-port {filter | shutdown} bpdu
Restricting Port Roles (Root Guard)
Loopback Detection (LBD) 
Configure the UDLD 
(UniDirectional Link Detection)
In case of a unidirectional link occurring 
on a link with a blocked port, you have a 
50 percent chance of a bridging loop.

<<<PAGE 188>>>
SPANNING TREE ADVANCED TROUBLESHOOTING
Factors to Consider When Choosing a Root Bridge
• What factors should you consider when choosing which switch should become 
the root bridge? 
• Location:
The root bridge should ideally be located at or near the "center" of your network to ensure
the shortest path to all other switches. This reduces the time it takes for STP control frames 
to travel across the network, thereby reducing STP convergence times.
• Processing Power and Memory:
The root bridge will handle more data than other switches, so it should have sufficient 
processing power and memory to manage this load without slowing down.
• Reliability:
As a critical component of your network, the root bridge should be a switch known for 
its reliability and low likelihood of failure.
• Network Design:
The choice of the root bridge also depends on your network design. 
For networks with multiple VLANs, you might need multiple root bridges 
at various locations, depending on where each VLAN is configured.

1.1. 
Correction: Issue: virtual chassis is not working correctly 
This a correction example with 6360-P24 switches. 
Reminder: 
 
- Upon boot-up, a switch will read its local vcsetup.cfg file and attempt to connect to the other neighbor 
switches  
- Upon connection, the switches will exchange the parameters configured in their local vcsetup.cfg files  
- As a result of this exchange, they will discover the topology, elect a Master  
- Start periodic health checks over the VFL, and synchronize their configuration as defined within the 
vcboot.cfg configuration file  
- All Slaves, if they do not have a local copy of vcboot.cfg, or if their local copy does not match the copy 
found on the Master, will download the vcboot.cfg from the Master chassis and reboot using this copy of 
vcboot.cfg as its configuration file  
 
A - Check type of switch used for the virtual chassis and port available to configure a virtual chassis 
a) On the switches 
 
 
Check on “OmniSwitch AOS Release 8 Switch Management Guide June 2025”  
 
6360-(P)24 
6360_(P)10 
(extract from switch management guide.pdf) 
(extract from OmniSwitch AOS Release 8 Specifications 
Guide.pdf)

4 
Troubleshoot LAB 2 
 
NOK_08 
    There are no virtual-fabric member ports configured on this switch. 
    If there are multiple virtual-fabric links configured, we must have 
    at least one member port configured or assigned to each of the 
    virtual-fabric links. 
    Troubleshooting Tips: 
    -> show virtual-chassis vf-link member-port | grep "<local-chassis-id>/" 
 
NOK_09 
    There are no virtual-fabric member interfaces operationally up. 
    If there are multiple virtual-fabric links configured, we must have 
    at least one member port interface up on each virtual-fabric link. 
    Troubleshooting Tips: 
    -> show virtual-chassis vf-link member-port | grep "<local-chassis-id>/" 
    -> show interfaces port <chassis>/<slot>/<port> status 
 
NOK_09 
    There are no virtual-fabric member interfaces operationally up. 
    If there are multiple virtual-fabric links configured, we must have 
    at least one member port interface up on each virtual-fabric link. 
    Troubleshooting Tips: 
    -> show virtual-chassis vf-link member-port | grep "<local-chassis-id>/" 
    -> show interfaces port <chassis>/<slot>/<port> status 
 
NOK_14 
    The virtual-fabric links configured on this switch are not operationally up. 
    If there are multiple links configured, all of them must be operationally up 
    in order for this parameter to be reported as OK. 
    Troubleshooting Tips: 
    -> show virtual-chassis vf-link | grep "<local-chassis-id>/" 
 
NOK_14 
    The virtual-fabric links configured on this switch are not operationally up. 
    If there are multiple links configured, all of them must be operationally up 
    in order for this parameter to be reported as OK. 
    Troubleshooting Tips: 
    -> show virtual-chassis vf-link | grep "<local-chassis-id>/" 
 
NOK_17 
    The virtual-chassis manager protocol did not discover any peer switch 
    within the discovery time window (i.e. 4 minutes) starting from the time 
    we reached the chassis ready state. 
    It is possible that there are no other peers, there are no virtual-fabric 
    links configured or operational between the switches, or the virtual-chassis 
    manager protocol packets are not going through. 
    Troubleshooting Tips: 
    a) Check if any peer switches were discovered using: 
       -> show virtual-chassis topology 
    b) If no peers were discovered, check whether the virtual-fabric links are 
       operational 
       -> show virtual-chassis vf-link member-port | grep "<local-chassis-id>/"

Layer 3 Troubleshooting – IP network problems

<<<PAGE 205>>>
Objectives
Lesson Summary
At the end of this module, you will be able to:
Perform monitoring and troubleshooting of IP Layer 3 
Protocols
IP network problems 
Packet lost via qos rules and port-monitoring
DHCP/UDP

<<<PAGE 206>>>
IP Troubleshooting

<<<PAGE 207>>>
Layer 3 Troubleshooting  : IP Troubleshooting
•
Main causes of IP network problems (non-exhaustive list)
Local Connectivity Problems
Configuration problem
DHCP or BOOTP issue
Physical layer issue
Duplicate IP address
First-hop Layer 3 connectivity 
issue, local LAN segment
Layer 3 IP connectivity issue 
somewhere along the packet's path
Name resolution issue
Routing loop
Routing protocol-related 
problem
A resource problem
(memory, buffers, CPU)
software problem might have 
occurred on a router
A packet filter or firewall might be 
preventing traffic from passing for 
an IP address or protocol
An MTU mismatch problem

<<<PAGE 208>>>
Layer 3 Troubleshooting  : IP Troubleshooting
•
Basic IP troubleshooting command
• show ip traffic
• show ip interface
• show ip routes
• show ip route-pref
• show ip redist
• show ip access-list
• show ip route-map
• show ip router database
• show ip emp-routes
• show ip config
• show ip protocols
• show ip router-id
• show ip service
• show ip service source-ip
The tools ping and traceroute, both in the TCP/IP protocol suite, will greatly assist in troubleshooting IP connectivity

Layer 3 Troubleshooting  : IP Troubleshooting
•
Advanced troubleshooting : IP Troubleshooting “Debug IP Packet”
•
Display internal traffic between the CMM and NIs :
->debug ip packet <start> <stop>
Must be used in combination with debug ip packet
Precaution must be taken when using the 
following commands
as it might dump a lot of information on the 
screen (restrict the traffic that it will display)
-> debug ip packet   { start [timeout <timeout>] } 
{ stop } 
{ direction <in | out | all> } 
{ format <header | hex | all>  } 
{ output <file | console> }
{ board { cmm |  ni | all | none }  } 
{ ether-type { arp | ip | hex <hex> | all }
{ ip-address <ip> } 
{ ip-pair <ip1>  <ip2> }  
{ protocol
{ tcp | udp | icmp | igmp | num <integer> | all }}  
{ show-broadcast <on | off>  }  
{ show-multicast <on | off> }
{ default }

 1 
Troubleshoot N°1 
1.1. 
Issue 
Both DHL link from 6360 to distribution are in dhl-blocking state? 
 
  20        0/7          tagged     forwarding 
  20        0/8          tagged     dhl-blocking 
  30        0/7          tagged     dhl-blocking 
  30        0/8          tagged     forwarding 
  57        0/7          untagged   dhl-blocking 
  58        0/8          untagged   dhl-blocking 
 
Troubleshoot this case and describe the procedure used to solve it. 
 2 
Troubleshoot N°2 
2.1. 
Introduction 
A DHCP server is available on training server to provides dynamic IP addresses on lease for client interfaces 
on a network. 
 
 
 
 
2.2. 
Issue 
Clients 5, 6, 9 and 10 can’t obtain an IP address and DNS server address automatically. 
Troubleshoot this case and describe the procedure used to do it.

Troubleshoot N°1 
1.1. 
Issue 
Client have disable DHL because when it is enabled 
Both DHL link from 6360 to distribution are in dhl-blocking state? 
 
  20        1/1/1        default    forwarding 
  20        0/7          qtagged    forwarding 
  20        0/8          qtagged    dhl-blocking 
  30        2/1/1        default    forwarding 
  30        0/7          qtagged    dhl-blocking 
  30        0/8          qtagged    forwarding 
  57        0/7          default    dhl-blocking 
  58        0/8          default    dhl-blocking 
  4001      1/1/24       default    forwarding 
 
Troubleshoot this case and describe the procedure used to do it. 
1.2. 
Solution 
 
Change the vlan on link 0/8 . Should be the same defaut (untagged) vlan on both link) 
 
sw8 (6860-B) -> sh vlan 57-58 members 
  vlan       port         type         status 
--------+------------+------------+-------------- 
  57        0/78         tagged     forwarding 
  58        0/8          untagged   forwarding

Typical RIP Error Conditions
General health of the switch and NI
First-hop Layer 3 connectivity 
issue, local LAN segment
Layer 3 IP connectivity issue 
somewhere along the packet's path
Routing loop
Routing protocol-related problem
Physical link status might not be operational
Route to the destination might not 
exist in either of the two routers
Subnet mask might not be correct on 
the edge device or the router
Forwarding flag set to No on the VLAN
RIP v1 and RIP v2 misinterpretation
RIP auth-type or auth-key misinterpretation
Configuration issue

<<<PAGE 233>>>
LAYER 3 PROTOCOL TROUBLESHOOTING
RIP v1 and RIP v2 misinterpretation
• Version compatibility
• If a RIP-2 router receives a RIP-1 Request
• it responds with a RIP-1 Response
• If the router is configured to send only RIP-2 messages
• it does not respond to a RIP-1 Request
• When a RIP-1 router receives information 
from a RIP-2 router 
• RIPv1 Routers ignore the v2 fields in a v2 packet 
(except if authentication is configured -> discard routes
• Physical link status might not be operational
• A network on an interface that is in shutdown 
will not be advertised.
• RIP auth-type or auth-key misinterpretation
• Both RIP routers should have the same authentication parameters

LAYER 3 PROTOCOL TROUBLESHOOTING
RIP operation: Route update
• If a route entry is not known, it is entered in the routing table
• If the route entry is already in the local routing table:
• If the new route has a lower hop count, the existing entry is replaced after the holddown timer expires
• Routes are directly flushed when routes are local and the local router interface goes down

OPEN SHORTEST PATH FIRST (OSPF)

<<<PAGE 238>>>
LAYER 3 PROTOCOL TROUBLESHOOTING - OSPF
Troubleshooting Typical OSPF Error Conditions 
• General health of the switch and NI
• Hello and Dead timer, Authentication password/type/key are mismatch between router
• Route to the destination might not exist in either of the two routers
• Subnet mask might not be correct on the edge device or the router
• Area mismatch on the interfaces
• Area type or id mismatch
• Subnet mask different on interfaces

<<<PAGE 239>>>
LAYER 3 PROTOCOL TROUBLESHOOTING - OSPF
OSPF Exchange Process reminder
R1
R2
Down State :Router have not exchanged any OSPF information 
Init State: A destination router has received a new router’s hello packet 
Adds it to its neighbor list 
2-Way State: The new router receives a unidirectional reply from the 
destination router
Adds the destination router to its neighbor list. Election of  DR&BDR
Exstart State: DR & BDR form adjacencies with the other OSPF routers
Exchange State: Sharing Link State information Database Description 
(DBD) packets which contains
• ID of the advertising router
• Cost of the advertising router
• Sequence number of the link
Loading State: Loading information in the Database (LSR, LSU, LSAck) 
Full State: Master & Slave synchronized > Incremental updates after 
entering a full state
- Hello interval: 10 seconds (keep-alive function)
- Dead interval: 40 seconds

OSPF Monitoring command
• Displays OSPF status and general configuration parameters
• Displays information regarding all or specified border routers
• Displays external Link State Advertisements from the areas to
which the router is attached.
The LSDB is the Link State Database
• Displays information on directly attached hosts
• Displays information on OSPF non-virtual neighbor routers
• Verifies the redistribution configuration
• Displays OSPF routes known to the switch
-> show ip ospf
-> show ip ospf border-routers
-> show ip ospf ext-lsdb
-> show ip ospf host
-> show ip ospf neighbor 
-> show ip redist
-> show ip ospf routes

LAYER 3 PROTOCOL TROUBLESHOOTING - OSPF
• Displays either all OSPF areas, or a specified OSPF area 
• Displays stub area status
• Displays LSAs in the Link State Database associated 
with each area
• Displays virtual link information.
A virtual link is used to connect OSPF areas that cannot
maintain a contiguous link through the backbone
• Displays OSPF virtual neighbors.
A virtual neighbor is connected to the switch via a 
virtual link rather than a physical one
-> show ip ospf area
-> show ip ospf area range 
-> show ip ospf area stub 
-> show ip ospf lsdb
-> show ip ospf virtual-link

<<<PAGE 249>>>
VIRTUAL ROUTER REDUNDANCY PROTOCOL 
(VRRP)

<<<PAGE 250>>>
VIRTUAL ROUTER REDUNDANCY PROTOCOL
• Timers
• Advertisement Interval 
• Time interval between VRRP Advertisements
• Make sure it is the same for all the VRID routers
• Skew_Time
• Time to keep routers from attempting to assume Master at the same time
• ( 256 - Priority) / 256
• Master_Down_Interval
• Time interval for the Backup to declare the Master down (in seconds)
• (3 * Advertisement_Interval ) + Skew_time
• Host ARP Requests
• When a host sends an ARP request for one of the virtual router IP Addresses, the Master Router 
must respond using the Virtual MAC Address (not its physical MAC Address)

<<<PAGE 258>>>
IP MULTICAST SWITCHING
• There should be 1 dedicated querier in the network (in case more than 1 querier is 
enabled, then one elected and the others remain inactive) 
• Querier-forwarding should be enabled only on switches located between multicast sources 
and the querier
• Zapping should be enabled only on edge devices 
• Proxying may be enabled on all devices 
• IGMP messages must be sent with TTL equal 1
->show ip multicast

DVMRP RECALL
• Multiple DVMRP routers on a subnet can lead to duplicate packets to be forwarded on the 
subnet (see drawing)
• If there are multiple DVMRP routers on a subnet
• One of the routers will be selected as the designated forwarder 
for each source/group pair based on route exchange reports
• Election by:
• Lowest metric
• As tie breaker, lowest IP address
• A DF is elected for each source 
• Multiple DFs on a subnet

4 
Troubleshoot LAB 4 
 
 3 
Troubleshoot N°3 
3.1. 
Introduction 
 
 
Client 1 is connected on VLAN 110. Port 1/1/1 on 6900-A –. 
Client 1 on 6860a is a multicast server (Security Camera). It sends the stream on Multicast group 231.1.1.1. 
 
Client 9 is connected on VLAN 30. Port 1/1/2 on 6360 VC –  
Client 9 is the receiver of the Security Camera. It should receive the stream on Multicast group 231.1.1.1 
3.2. 
Issue 
Multicast source: Client 1 - Stream 231.1.1.1 -video Monitoring 
Reception: client 9 
 
Client 1 can ping client 9 but multicast traffic is not routed to client 9. 
Troubleshoot this case and describe the procedure used to do it.

<<<PAGE 290>>>
10 
Troubleshoot LAB 4 
 
 3 
Troubleshoot N°3 - solution 
3.1. 
Introduction 
 
 
Client 1 is connected on VLAN 110. Port 1/1/1 on 6900-A –. 
Client 1 on 6900a is a multicast server (Security Camera). It sends the stream on Multicast group 231.1.1.1. 
 
Client 9 is connected on VLAN 30. Port 1/1/2 on 6360 VC –  
Client 9 is the receiver of the Security Camera. It should receive the stream on Multicast group 231.1.1.1 
3.2. 
Issue 
Multicast source: Client 1 - Stream 231.1.1.1 -video Monitoring 
Reception: client 9 
 
Client 1 can ping client 9 but multicast traffic is not routed to client 9. 
Troubleshoot this case and describe the procedure used to do it.

<<<PAGE 291>>>
11 
Troubleshoot LAB 4 
 
3.3. 
Solution 
 
Try to ping from client 9 to client 1. 
Check unicast route used from 6360-A to join multicast server (client 1) 
 
sw5 (6360-A) -> traceroute 192.168.110.50 
traceroute to 192.168.110.50 (192.168.110.50), 30 hops max, 60 byte packets 
 1   (192.168.57.7)  2.257 ms  3.766 ms  3.638 ms 
 2   (172.16.17.1)  1.515 ms  1.157 ms  1.406 ms 
 3   (192.168.110.50)  1.270 ms  1.160 ms  1.334 ms 
 
- The best path to the source is given in the unicast routing table. 
- The join request is sent on this interface. 
- It is propagated to the source. 
- Each router creates an (S,G) state in its multicast routing table 
 
- The multicast flow propagates from the source to the receiver, along the path thus created. 
- Check unicast route used from 6360-A to join multicast server (client 1) 
 
sw7 (6870-A) -> sh ip pim interface

--------------------------------+---------------+---------------+--------+--------+--------+--------

int_217                          172.16.17.7     172.16.17.7     30       60       enabled  disabled 
int_20                           192.168.20.7    192.168.20.8    30       60       enabled  disabled 
int_278                          172.16.78.7     172.16.78.8     30       60       enabled  disabled 
int_30                           192.168.30.7    192.168.30.8    30       60       enabled  disabled

                                                 Router          Interval Interval Status   Status 
--------------------------------+---------------+---------------+--------+--------+--------+-------- 
int_212                          172.16.12.1     172.16.12.2     30       60       enabled  disabled 
int-110                          192.168.110.1   192.168.110.1   30       60       enabled  disabled

<<<PAGE 292>>>
12 
Troubleshoot LAB 4 
 
So, we must enable PIM-SM on the necessary interfaces. 
 
 sw1 (6900-A) -> ip pim interface int_217 
 
Manage the client 1 and 9 to send and receive multicast traffic as indicated in the tables below 
 
PC Client 
Send 
Receive 
Client 1 
Grps: 231.1.1.1 
 
Client 9 
 
Grps: 231.1.1.1 
.

<<<PAGE 294>>>
Objectives
Lesson Summary
At the end of this module, you will be able to:
Apply the monitoring and troubleshooting for:
• QoS / ACL
• UNP

<<<PAGE 295>>>
Quality of Service

<<<PAGE 296>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
• REMINDER
• Decide which traffic needs preferential treatment and 
which traffic can be adequately served with best effort
• HOW IT WORKS
• QoS is implemented on the switch through the use of :
- Port-based QoS configuration
- User-defined policies
- Integration with virtual output queuing to manage egress 
congestion

Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
Description
Command/keyword
By default QoS is enabled on the switch. If QoS policies are 
configured and applied, the switch attemps to classify and apply
relevant policy actions
qos enable/disable
Displays global information about QoS configuration
show qos config
Resets the QoS configuration to its defaults. 
qos reset
Deletes any QoS configuration that has not been applied to the 
configuration through the qos apply command. 
qos revert
Deletes all pending policy information. This command is different 
from qos revert, which returns the pending policy configuration to 
its last applied settings. 
qos flush
Applies configured global QoS and policy settings to the current 
configuration (changes will be active and stored in flash). 
qos apply

<<<PAGE 300>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
•
The QoS software in the switch creates its own log for QoS-specific events.
-
The debug qos command controls what kind of information is displayed in the log.
-
The qos log level command determines how specific the log messages are. 
info 
Logs basic information about the switch. 
config 
Logs information about the global configuration. 
rule 
Logs events for rules configured on the switch. 
main 
Logs information about basic program interfaces. 
port 
Logs events related to QoS ports. 
msg 
Logs QoS messages. 
sl
Logs information about source learning. 
mem 
Logs information about memory. 
mapper 
Logs information about mapping queues. 
slot 
Logs events related to slots. 
l2 
Logs Layer 2 QoS events on the switch. 
l3 
Logs Layer 3 QoS events on the switch. 
classifier 
Logs information whenever the switch classifies a flow; more details are provided if the log level is higher. 
sem
Logs information about semaphore, process locking. 
pm 
Logs events related to the Policy Manager. 
ingress 
Logs information about packets arriving on the switch. 
egress 
Logs information about packets leaving the switch.
By default basic information 
messages are logged (info). Error 
messages are always logged. 
-> debug qos rule

<<<PAGE 301>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
•
debug qos internal 
•
Displays debugging information for QoS internal to the switch.
flow 
Displays information about QoS flows. 
queue 
Displays information about QoS queues. 
port 
Displays information about QoS ports. 
l2tree 
Displays information about Layer 2 flows. 
l3tree 
Displays information about Layer 3 flows. 
vector 
Displays information about vectors. 
pending 
Displays information about pending QoS objects. 
verbose 
Sets the output to verbose mode for more detailed information. 
mapper 
Displays information about QoS mapping flows to queues. 
pool 
Displays information about the buffer pool. 
log 
Displays information about QoS information that is logged. 
pingonly
Specifies that any policies configured with an ICMP protocol condition apply only to ICMP echo-requests and 
echo-replies. 
nopingonly
Configures the switch so that any policies configured with an ICMP protocol condition apply to any ICMP 
packets. 
-> debug qos internal "verbose log"

<<<PAGE 302>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
•
Log for QoS-specific events
•
QoS log displays a maximum of 10000 lines. To change the maximum number of lines that can display
•
Log Detail Level
•
The log level determines the amount of detail that is given in the QoS log
•
The qos log level command is associated with the qos debug command, which determines what kind of information is included in 
the log.
•
The default log level is 5. The range of values is 1 (lowest level of detail) to 8 (highest level of detail). For
-> qos log lines 30
-> qos apply
->  qos log level 7
->  qos apply

<<<PAGE 303>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
•
Forwarding Log Events to the Console
•
QoS log messages can be sent to the switch logging utility, which is an event logging application available on the 
OmniSwitch.
-
The configuration of the switch logging utility then determines if QoS messages are sent to a log file in the switch’s flash file system, displayed on the 
switch console, and/or sent to a remote syslog server.
•
Displaying the QoS Log
-> qos log console
-> qos apply
sw1 (6900-A) -> show qos log
**QOS Log**

<<<PAGE 304>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
•
Clearing the QoS Log
. The QoS log can get large if invalid rules are configured on the switch, or if a lot of QoS events have taken place.  
Clearing the log makes the file easier to manage.
•
Setting the Statistics Interval
•
To change how often the switch polls the network interfaces for QoS statistics, use the qos stats interval command 
with the desired interval time in seconds. The default is 60 seconds.
•
Statistics are displayed through
-> clear qos log
> qos stats interval 30
> show qos statistics

<<<PAGE 305>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
•
Remote Port Mirroring (RPM)
•
Allows traffic to be carried over the network to a remote switch
•
Achieved by using a dedicated remote port mirroring VLAN 
•
RPM VLAN has to be configured on the source, destination and intermediate switches 
•
No other traffic is allowed on that VLAN
The following types of traffic will not be mirrored:
- Link Aggregation Control Packets (LACP), 802.1AB (LLDP), 802.1x port authentication,  802.3ag (OAM), Layer 3 
control packets, Generic Attribute Registration Protocol (GARP)
SOURCE PORT
DESTINATION PORT
INTERMEDIATE SWITCH
SOURCE SWITCH
DESTINATION SWITCH

<<<PAGE 306>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
•
Policy Based Mirroring
•
Mirroring is done based on a QoS policy instead of a specific port
1 session supported at any given time
•
Port Based Mirroring. It can be done on incoming or outgoing traffic or both.
policy action mirror
•
Mirror traffic based on
Source & Destination addresses
Address pairs
Protocols
VLAN classification
•
Port mirroring and monitoring cannot be configured on the same port
MIRRORING POLICY
INGRESS, EGRESS, OR BOTH INGRESS & EGRESS PACKETS
POLICY ACTION & PORT ASSIGNMENT
DIRECT TRAFFIC TO MIRROR PORT

<<<PAGE 307>>>
Quality of Service -Troubleshoot
•
Policy Based Mirroring 
•
Example 1
- -> policy condition c1 source ip 1.1.1.1
- -> policy action a1 ingress egress mirror 1/1/1
- -> policy rule r1 condition c1 action a1
- -> qos apply
- Policy rule r1 will cause all packets with a source IP of 1.1.1.1 to be ingress and egress mirrored to port 1/1/1
•
Example 2
- -> policy condition c1 source ip 1.1.1.1
- -> policy action a2 ingress egress mirror 1/1/1 disposition drop
- -> policy rule r2 condition c1 action a2
- -> qos apply
- Policy rule r2 drops traffic with a source IP of 1.1.1.1, but the mirrored traffic from this source is not dropped and 
is forwarded to port 1/1/1

and check that the user has sudo rights by logging in to

support if desired.

Step 2 – Package Installation on Red Hat Enterprise Linux
Use SFTP to transfer the .rpm application file to the Red Hat host.

Follow the prompts regarding IP addresses, proxy, and TLS for syslog support if desired.

Deactivate firewalld with the command:
$ systemctl disable firewalld –now
Required for the k3s files (from OVNA) to be later installed on the VM.

1.2. 
Lab Setup 
The switch used for this lab is an AOS 6900A. An OmniVista Network Advisor server has already been installed 
and switch has been setup with some configuration so it can be reachable from OVNA. 
We will simulate two use cases: DDOS attack and crash dump on switch 6900A.  
To simulate them, we will use a Linux command to provide an easy way to add messages log from the 
command line to the switch. Switch will send this log via syslog to the server.  
We will see the process on how an anomaly is reported in OVNA and a notification message is set to a 
Rainbow bubble for the user to determine an action to be taken regarding the issue.

1.7. 
Manage devices. 
- Select Device Management from the left menu. 
The Device Management screen is used to manage devices that Network Advisor is monitoring in your 
network.  
This screen is divided into two sections: Monitored Devices and Protected IPs. 
There are two options for adding the devices you want Network Advisor to monitor: 
You can add a single device, 
Or import a list of devices. 
We will add a single device to monitor, click on Add device at the top-right corner of the List of Monitored 
Devices tab. 
 
 
 
- Complete the following in the Add and configure a new device window 
 
Host Name :6900A 
Ip Address: 192.168.254.1 
Device Type: OmniSwitch LAN 
SSH PORT: 22 
Device login: admin 
Password: switch 
Category: My-AOS 
 
Click Submit. 
 
 
Notes

Troubleshoot and monitor an OmniSwitch using Network Advisor 
 
 
 
 
You should see now the 6900A switch in the Monitored devices list. 
 
 
Notes 
The status of the switch may be shown as Pending until the next call home when it then changes to Online 
 
 
 
Every time a new switch is added to OVNA, or when for an existing device the Syslog port or application 
FQDN/IP is changed, OVNA automatically pushes the associated syslog commands to the OmniSwitch.  
- Check the show swlog command on the OmniSwitch 6900A 
- Look for the Log device 2 entry. It should be shown as displayed below:  
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> show swlog 
Operational Status                 : Running, 
File Size per file                 : 1250 Kbytes, 
Log Device 1                       : console flash socket, 
Log Device 2                       : ipaddr 192.168.100.103:10514, 
Syslog FacilityID                  : local0(16), 
Hash Table entries age limit       : 60 seconds, 
Switch Log Preamble                : Enabled, 
Switch Log Debug                   : Disabled, 
Switch Log Duplicate Detection     : Enabled, 
Console Display Level              : info, 
RFC5424 Format Logging             : Disabled, 
Swlog Threshold                    : 90 percent, 
Swlog over TLS                     : Disabled, 
Output Socket Console              : Disabled, 
Swlog Default Level                : info, 
 
- If the switch does not have this entry, then use the following commands: 
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> swlog output socket 192.168.100.103 10514 vrf-name default  
sw1 (6900-A) -> interfaces ddm enable  
sw1 (6900-A) -> interfaces ddm-trap enable 
 
- Th     l c          x  c          c     Sy l            f        c ’  IP         c  f           h  D   c  
Management table. 
- For this lab, we are using the 192.168.254.1 IP address, which corresponds to the Loopback0 address. 
 
If a switch is running with several IP interfaces, you can force the Syslog packets to be generated from a 
specific IP interface.

 2 
Use Case 1: DDos Attack 
- Check the port 1/1/1 status. It should be enabled. 
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> sh interfaces 1/1/1 status 
 Chas/                 DETECTED-VALUES              CONFIGURED-VALUES 
 Slot/    Admin  Auto  Speed   Duplex  Pause  FEC   Speed   Duplex  Pause  FEC   Link 
 Port     Status Nego  (Mbps)                 Det   (Mbps)                 Cfg   Trap  EEE 
---------+------+----+--------+------+-------+----+--------+------+-------+-----+-----+--- 
 1/1/1       en    en    1000   Full     -     DIS   Auto    Auto     -    AUTO  en   dis 
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> sh vlan members port 1/1/1 
  vlan      type        status 
--------+-----------+--------------- 
   110    untagged    forwarding 
 
We are going to simulate a DDOS attack on the switch. For that, we will use a specific LINUX command. 
The logger command provides an easy way to add log entries from the command line to the switch to make 
tests .We will use an example of a generated log entry in case of DDOS attack. 
From super user maintenance shell, we run the logger command and check that a log entry is added to the 
switch. 
This is an example of a log entry received on the switch in case of DDOS attack: 
OS6900A -s swlogd ipni dos WARN: VRF 0: DoS type ping overload from 10.130.7.124\/54:5f:50:b0:6d:7b on 
port 1\/1\/1 
 
- Type the following on switch 6900A: 
 
Note : type the command logger -t on the terminal and then copy and paste the associated log text 
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> su 
Entering maintenance shell. Type 'exit' when you are done. 
YUKON #-> logger –t swlogd ipni dos WARN: VRF 0: DoS type ping overload from 
10.130.7.124\/54:5f:50:b0:6d:7b on port 1\/1\/1  
YUKON #-> exit 
logout 
 
- Check log entry on the switch  
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> show log swlog |grep overload 
2024 Jan 23 17:16:31.252 swlogd ipni dos WARN: VRF 0: DoS type ping overload from 
10.130.7.124/54:5f:50:b0:6d:7b on port 1/1/1 
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> show log swlog |grep 1/1/1 
2024 Jan 23 17:16:31.252 swlogd ipni dos WARN: VRF 0: DoS type ping overload from 
10.130.7.124/54:5f:50:b0:6d:7b on port 1/1/1 
 
- Check Rainbow Bubble notification. 
 
A notification message with log entry information including the OmniSwitch IP Address/Hostname, Mac 
Address and interface/port number is sent to the Rainbow bubble defined during the installation. 
- In OVNA, Go to Rainbow Bubble Management. 
- Look for the OVNA PodX bubble in the Rainbow Bubble list. 
- Click on Link to bubble.

 3 
Use case 2: Core Dump 
Simulate a log on the switch: 
We are going to simulate a switch crash that generates a PMD file on the switch.  
For that, we will use a specific Linux command to add a log entry message to the switch log. 
- This is an example of log entry received on the switch in case of switch crash that generates a PMD file.  
 
OS6900A -s swlogd PMD main ALRT: PMD generated at \/flash\/pmd\/pmd-etherCmm-11.04.2022-14.54.53 
 
- Type the following on switch 6900A: 
 
Note : type the command logger -t on the terminal and then copy and paste the associated log text 
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> su 
Entering maintenance shell. Type 'exit' when you are done. 
YUKON #-> logger -t OS6900A -s swlogd PMD main ALRT: PMD generated at \/flash\/pmd\/pmd-etherCmm-
11.07.2024-14.54.533  
YUKON #-> exit 
 
 
 
- Check the log entry generated on the switch. 
 
sw1 (6900-A) -> show log swlog |grep pmd 
2024 Jan 23 17:30:39.048 OS6860N swlogd PMD main ALRT: PMD generated at /flash/pmd/pmd-etherCmm-
11.04.2022-14.54.533 
 
- Check Rainbow Bubble notification. 
- A notification message is sent including the OmniSwitch IP Address/Hostname. 
- The notification proposes to collect log & PMD files and invites you to contact ALE Customer Support Team 
 
 
 
- Check the OVNA Web User Interface information related to the Anomaly entry.

- Check on the OVNA Web User Interface information related to this Anomaly

3 
OVNA Installation 
 
 1 
Prerequisites 
Knowledge of the following topics are required to install OmniVista Cirrus Network Advisor: 
• 
Ubuntu/Debian distribution 
• 
Red Hat Enterprise Linux distribution 
• 
Virtual Machines management 
 
OmniVista Network Advisor is installed on a Virtual Machine running either on Ubuntu, Debian or Red Hat EL. 
Knowledge of these distributions, and how to manage and operate Virtual Machines hosts is required. 
 2 
Get the latest Software package 
2.1. 
MyPortal 
 
In MyPortal, look for the latest version of Network Advisor, and download the software package.  
Download either the Debian package for an installation on an Ubuntu or Debian distribution. 
Or the RPM package for an installation on a Red Hat EL distribution. 
 
 
Use the following link to reach the OmniVista Network Advisor software page: 
https://al-enterprise.lightning.force.com/lightning/r/Taxonomy__c/a6f5I000000Xf6kQAC/view

<<<PAGE 389>>>
4 
OVNA Installation 
 
2.2. 
Unzip the file 
 
Unzip the package you have just downloaded. 
You will get the Debian or RPM file. Here is an example of the Debian package unzipped: 
 
We will see how to upload the Debian or RPM file to the virtual machine in the next section. 
 3 
Server VM Installation 
3.1. 
System Requirements 
 
The following are the suggested minimum requirements for installing and running OmniVista Network Advisor: 
 
• Processor: Quad-core  
• RAM: 8 GB  
• HDD: Up to 200 devices, 50 GB (To allow for storage of syslog messages) 
For 1,000 devices, 120GB storage is recommended 
 
For 2,000 devices, 210GB storage is recommended  
• Linux OS: Ubuntu 22.04 Server Edition, Debian 11 or 12, or Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9.3  
   • Ports:  
TCP 80/443 (HTTP/S for WebUI),  
UDP/TCP 10514 
TCP with secure TLS 6514 (syslog over TLS),  
TCP 22 (SSH/SFTP Application to devices or access to Application),  
TCP 443 (Setup Installation and Cloud Services)  
    • Internet access is mandatory.  
 
3.2. 
Ubuntu server installation 
 
3.2.1. 
Ubuntu Server installation on VMware ESXi 
 
Download the supported version of Ubuntu 22.04 
https://ubuntu.com/download/server

<<<PAGE 390>>>
5 
OVNA Installation 
 
On your Vmware ESXi, go to File > New > Virtual Machine 
 
 
 
Define the VM Name and Location 
 
 
Select the Database storage. 
 
 
 
Specify the required Guest OS. For OVNA, Ubuntu Linux is selected.

<<<PAGE 391>>>
6 
OVNA Installation 
 
Specify the number of network interfaces required. For OVNA, only 1 NIC card is required. 
 
 
 
All selected settings can be reviewed and then the New Virtual Machine is created. 
 
Make sure that the VM is powered ON. Select the VM and right-click Power > Power On. 
 
Select the VM and click on Connect / Disconnect CD / DVD Devices (CD icon) > Connect to ISO image on 
local disk…

Installation of OVNA 
4.1. 
Configure User Management Account for Ubuntu/Debian 
 
Under root account type the following (user account requirements may differ based on installed platform):  
 
$ usermod -aG sudo <username>  
 
Check that the user has sudo rights by logging in to the user session and typing sudo -v. 
 
4.2. 
Package installation 
4.2.1. 
Debian package installation 
 
Go to the directory where the Debian package has been uploaded. Install the package.  
 
$ sudo dpkg -i ale-ovna.deb  
 
 
4.2.2. 
RPM package installation 
 
Go to the directory where the RPM package has been uploaded. Install the package.  
 
$ sudo rpm -ivh ale-ovna.rpm

OVNA installation 
 
For either rpm or dedbian package 
$ sudo ale-ovna install 
 
 
Select the IP address that will be used for Omnivista Network Advisor if multiple interfaces were configured. 
Define if a proxy configuration will be used 
Define if syslog over TLS will be supported 
 
 
 
The service downloads the required packages starting with ‘k3s’ and continues with the deployment of the 
downloaded images. The deployment time will vary depending on Internet connectivity, server bandwidth 
and other factors. The progress is updated as the components are installed. 
 
 
 
Once the installation is complete a connection can be made to the Web application to start the setup or 
restore a previous backup of an installation.

$ sudo rpm -ivh ale-ovna-package-1.4.35.rpm ?????? 
$ sudo ale-ovna update  
  
5.2. 
OVNA uninstallation 
 
To uninstall your OVNA, use the following commands: 
 
$ sudo ale-ovna uninstall

Objectives 
This document will guide you through the configuration of your Microsoft Teams 
environment to prepare the interaction with OmniVista Network Advisor. 
Follow the setup guide before installing OmniVista Network Advisor. The MS Teams 
configuration will provide some information and IDs required during OmniVista Network 
advisor setup.  
Audience 
Any people involved in the deployment of OmniVista Network Advisor and Microsoft 
Teams: 
• ALE partners 
• Customers IT service, administrators, field engineers 
• ALE Technical Support 
• ALE Technical support 
Prerequisites 
• Microsoft Company account 
All the steps described in this document need to be executed with a Microsoft 
company account (with administrator rights on a company)  
 
• Microsoft License 
Get a Microsoft license for a product which comes with at least Microsoft Entra 
ID.  
Such products include, but are not limited to: 
o Standalone Microsoft Entra P1, P2 
o Standalone Microsoft Entra Suite 
o Microsoft 365 E3, E5 
o Microsoft 365 Business Premium 
 
• Public URL to reach OmniVista Network Advisor  
To enable Microsoft Teams to access your Network Advisor application, a public 
URL with a specified port must be exposed on the Internet.  
Managing the exposure of this public URL and port is the client's responsibility, 
including configuring the route and port to ensure accessibility on customer  
Ex : https://omnivista.network-advisor.yourdomain.com:10510/msteams

Version 1.4 
December 2, 2024 
4 
 
This is the way MS Teams will inform OmniVista Network Advisor that an action is 
required following a notification. 
Ex: Apply remediation, Collect Logs, 
Acknowledge… 
 
 
 
 
Phase 1 of 4: Creating the Entra App and Teams Bot  
You will need to create an App registered in Entra ID and link a Teams Bot to this app. 
Then you will be able to add the bot to your team channel to send anomaly notifications. 
 
Step 1 of 6: Go to Microsoft Teams’s dev page (https://dev.teams.microsoft.com/)  
and login with your company’s admin account.

name of your bot in MS Teams, so choose an easily identifiable name. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Step 5 of 6: Add your bot endpoint 
This is the URL at which your bot will be hosted. It will also receive messages and 
requests from Microsoft Teams through that address. 
Make sure the endpoint is publicly accessible and follows this format: 
Ex: https://omnivista.network-advisor.yourdomain.com/msteams  
If your bot is hosted locally, you can use tunneling tools like ngrok to create a temporary 
public URL for testing purposes. This URL will later need to be replaced with the final 
production endpoint when the bot is deployed. 
Note: Configuring and managing the exposure of the route and port to make the 
endpoint publicly accessible is the client's responsibility. 
Then click “Save.”

Phase 4 of 4: Enable Graph API authorizations 
To allow the application to manage Teams and channels (e.g., creating and deleting 
them) through the user interface, you will need to enable Graph API permissions. A 
detailed list of the required permissions is provided below. 
Step 1 of 3: From the previous page, click “API permissions” in the sidebar. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Then click “Add a permission.”

<<<PAGE 418>>>
Microsoft Teams Bot Setup Guide  
Version 1.4 
December 2, 2024 
16 
 
Step 2 of 3: Choose “Microsoft Graph” then select “Application 
permission.” 
Step 3 of 3: Manually add all permissions for the app. 
 
These are the permissions you want:  
− 
Team.ReadBasic.All: Read basic information on all teams. 
− 
TeamSettings.Read.All: Read team settings. 
− 
TeamSettings.ReadWrite.All: Read and modify team settings. 
− 
Group.ReadWrite.All: Manage Microsoft 365 groups (read, create, modify, delete). 
− 
Channel.Create: Create channels in teams. 
− 
Channel.Delete: Delete channels in teams. 
− 
ChannelSettings.ReadWrite.All: Read and modify channel settings. 
− 
User.Read.All: Read organization user information. 
− 
Directory.ReadWrite.All: Read and modify Azure AD directory information. 
− 
Member.ReadWrite.All: Manage members in groups and teams. 
 
PRO TIP: When adding these permissions, you can simplify the process: 
1. Search for a permission: Copy/paste a permission name in the search bar

<<<PAGE 419>>>
Microsoft Teams Bot Setup Guide  
Version 1.4 
December 2, 2024 
17 
 
2. Expand all: Click “expand all” in the top right corner of the search bar 
3. Select everything: Make sure to tick every checkbox matching the query 
4. Go back to Step 1: Replace your search query with the next permission in the list 
(all previously selected permissions will be retained) 
5. Submit: Repeat until all permissions have been checked, then click “Add 
permissions” 
6. Apply your changes: You must finally click “Grand admin consent for (your 
organization)” to confirm your changes

<<<PAGE 423>>>
Design Process

<<<PAGE 424>>>
Design Process
Relates to new network deployment, network modifications 
and expansions.
Requires:
●An understanding of the requirements (understanding of the 
problem is 80% of the solution)
●An understanding of the available technologies
●Experience in the effective use of technologies to solve 
problems
Uses a structured method 
●“divide and conquer” philosophy
●Break the project down into manageable pieces
●By focusing on the details of each piece, you can ensure a more 
complete and effective design
Does not include:
●Installation
●Reconfiguration
●Troubleshooting
●Ongoing Administrative solutions (e.g., alarms management, 
procedures and reporting)

<<<PAGE 425>>>
Design Process Goals
Any design is compromised between:
Business / Application Growth
Reliability
"The network exists to support applications.
Applications exist to fulfill business 
requirements."
Response Time
Cost / ROI

Identify Customer Needs
Determine business goals and limitations

• Availability

• Manageability
- Performance, faults, configuration, security, accounting
• Affordability
- Maximizing effectiveness vs. cost

<<<PAGE 444>>>
Architecture Concepts 
Hierarchical models for internetwork design
• Network can be segmented in layers 
- Offers hierarchy – each layer has specific role
- Modular topology - building blocks
- Easy to grow, understand, and troubleshoot
- Creates small fault domains (clear demarcations and isolation)
- Promotes load balancing and redundancy
- Promotes deterministic traffic patterns
- Incorporates balance of both Layer 2 and Layer 3 technology, leveraging the strength of both
- Uses Layer 3 Routing for load balancing, fast convergence, scalability, and control
- Uses Layer 3 switching in distribution and backbone and Layer 2+ switching in wiring closet
Physical Network
Test
Documentation
Audit
Logical Network

<<<PAGE 445>>>
Architecture Concepts
2 Tier vs. 3 Tier
• 2 Tier High-throughput 
- High Performance, non-blocking design, High Density
- Dense deployment, Switch & link optimization
- Faultless with low and predictable latency (1.5 to 6µs)
- Both layers provide L2 & L3 Switching
- Access and distribution layers merging 
Data Center – Servers with built-in switches
Enterprise – Fewer management points, less STP
• 3 Tier Scalable Segmentation, 
- Flexible, low cost, Medium Density  
- Separate devices provide L2 & L3 Switching
- Reliability, Redundancy, Fault Tolerance, 
- Manageability, Efficiency, Adaptability, (Lower Cost)
- low-latency (>12µs)
Physical Network
Test
Documentation
Audit
Logical Network

<<<PAGE 448>>>
Three Layer Design
Core
• Maximum throughput capability
- High capacity backplanes
- High throughput switching
• High availability
- Redundant P.S, Switching HW and Links
Aggregation
• Delineates broadcast domains
- Routes between VLANs
• Deals with diverse characteristics of the access 
sites
- Devices should have a wide range of media
• Employs routing protocol redistribution, route 
summarization and default routes to optimize the 
routing functions
Access
• Connects users on local networks to the network
• Bandwidth is allocated to user groups and 
applications
• QOS/Security and filtering measures need to be 
applied at this layer, if necessary
• Main area for controlling the size of the broadcast 
domains.
• Access from small remote areas requires WAN 
connections and backup links

<<<PAGE 451>>>
Two Layer Design
Core
• Maximum throughput capability
- High-capacity backplanes
- High throughput switching
• Avoids using filtering and other policy-based 
functions
- Wide range of media, protocol and upper-layer 
networking functionality
• Employs routing protocol redistribution, route 
summarization and default routes to optimize the 
routing functions
• High availability
- Redundant P.S
- Redundant Switching HW
- Redundant Links
Access
• Connects users on local networks to the 
internetwork
• Bandwidth is allocated to user groups and 
applications
• QOS/Security and filtering measures need to be 
applied at this layer, if necessary
• The main area for controlling the size of the 
broadcast domains.

<<<PAGE 496>>>
Availability and Resiliency

<<<PAGE 497>>>
Availability 
Carrier Class Availability consists of…
• No traffic interruption due to HW/SW failure or SW loads
• Non-stop operation and no loss of capacity when management module fails 
• On-line card swapping
• Load sharing 
• Network recovery 
• Network monitoring
At the edge not only at the core
• Users require full availability at the edge
• Data centers require highest availability
Amount of time in which the network is available
• Expressed in % uptime
• Time unit can be expressed in years, months, weeks, days or hours 
• Length of outage

<<<PAGE 498>>>
Resiliency 
Resiliency can be defined as:
• How quickly the network can be restored
- Automated fault recovery whenever backup links and devices are used
• Elimination of  single point of failure
Switch resiliency 
• Hardware (NI, Power Supply, Fabric, Links, …)
• System (File System, non-stop operation, …)
Network resiliency  
• Layer 2 (STP, LACP, DHL, …) and Layer 3 (OSPF, VRRP, …)
• Application (SLB)
Resiliency at all cost?
• Extra equipment = extra cost. Cost-balance benefits?
- Do not backup a backup.
• Adds complexity to the network

<<<PAGE 499>>>
Hardware Resiliency 
Stacking
• Virtual chassis, single IP for management
• Primary, secondary, idle and pass-through elements in the stack
• Stack module IDs are set using CLI and displayed on the panel
• Each module in the stack is capable to act as Primary
• MAC Retention

<<<PAGE 510>>>
Dual Home Link Active-Active
Operation
DHL Active-Active splits a number of VLANs between two active links
One session per switch is allowed
• Two DHL links associated with the session (link A and link B)
• Group of VLANs in which each VLAN is associated (802.1q tagged) with both link A and link B
• VLAN-to-link mapping specifying which of the common VLANs each DHL link will service

DHL Network Loops
Forwarding status of each VLAN is modified by DHL to 
prevent network loops and maintain connectivity to the 
core when one of the links fails
Link between uplink device other than core network is not 
advisable as it will create loop
Solution on Access switches
• LPS, Loop Guard, BPDU Shutdown

Virtual Chassis - Takeover/Failover
Only master reloads, no impact on slaves, no traffic impact except related to master
When the master reloads or fails, the slaves reelect a new master 
New master election is locally computed based on known partner keys
The new master will confirm its slaves the decision

<<<PAGE 527>>>
Virtual Chassis - Split Chassis
Failures on VFL links cause potential MAC/IP  duplication
2 mechanisms
• Out of Band: EMP Remote Chassis Detection (RCD)
• In Band: VC Split Protocol
Virtual Chassis
Master
Slave
Master

<<<PAGE 528>>>
Virtual Chassis - Split Chassis
Setup EMP network to allow distinction between a switch down and VFL trunk down
EMP Remote Chassis Detection (RCD) facility is always running on every switch
Each switch sends / receives RCD announcements to and from its peers over the EMP port
A switch sends an announcement whenever its chassis VC information changes
RCD use the following IP addresses in order of preference
1. CMM IP address stored in NVRAM (if configured)
2. Chassis EMP IP address

ERPv2

<<<PAGE 542>>>
ERPv2
• Supports a maximum of 64 ERP rings
Major Ring
⚫Controls a full physical ring and is connected to the Interconnection Nodes on two ports
•
ERP 1 ring composed of A-B-C-E-D-A
•
Major Ring is controlled by its own ERP instance with its own RPL
Sub-Ring
⚫Connects to a Major Ring at the Interconnection Nodes
•
Sub-Ring does not constitute a closed ring
•
ERP 2 ring composed of D-F-H-G-E
•
A Sub-Ring is connected to the Interconnection nodes on only one port
•
Sub-Ring is controlled by its own ERP instance with its own RPL 
Interconnection Nodes
⚫Ring nodes that are common to both interconnected rings
⚫Nodes D and E are the interconnection nodes

<<<PAGE 551>>>
Security
Controlling user access
Protection from unintentional or malicious network infrastructure corruption
Carrier Class Security consists of…
• Controlling access to the network, the infrastructure and resources
• At the switch, through the switch and between the switch protection
• Isolating user groups
• Defending against employee snooping, hostile intrusion and DOS attacks
• Provide configuration and administration granularity
Distributed Multi-layer security

<<<PAGE 552>>>
Security at the Switch
Regulation of switch admin based on user ID – AAA service 
-
Local switch database
-
Remote authentication - RADIUS, LDAP, RSA
-
Privilege-based (Partitioned Management)
Accounting records maintained when using remote AAA database
HW based mechanism to secure the Control plane
- Integrated DoS protection (early discard) & L2 Storm Control
Switch protocol security
- SSH, SFTP, HTTPS, MD5 for RIPv2, OSPFv2 and SNMPv3 
IP services selective management
Benefits
• Management control
• Provides least privilege / need-to-know access rights
• Reduces admin effort in large networks

Security through the Switch
Learned Port Security (LPS)
• Provides a mechanism for authorizing source learning of MAC addresses on 10/100 and Gigabit Ethernet 
ports
Objectives 
• Limit the amount of time source learning occurs on all LPS ports
• Limit the max number of L2 addresses that can be learned on a port. (Dynamic or Static)
• Limit the L2 address learning for the specific period of time

Security through the switch
Policy-based Routing
• Policy-based Routing is a way to define other policies to override the normal routing mechanism
• Policy type based on
- Source or destination IP address
- Source or destination network group
- Source or destination TCP/UDP port
- Service or service group
- IP protocol
- Built-in source port group
• Action
- Route to this gateway no matter what the routing table indicates

Performance/QoS
The ability to tailor the network performance based on users, services or resources types 
regardless of location
Performance is not only horsepower
• Wirespeed services is what matters not just capacity
• There are always bottleneck in a network (servers access, WAN access, aggregation points ) however 
mission critical and delay sensitive applications need to be ensured to get through
QoS consists of…
• No feature or performance bottleneck
• Wirespeed everything
• Data prioritization
• Bandwidth management
• Congestion control
• 10-Gig ready

<<<PAGE 577>>>
Performance
Wire-speed everything - no service performance 
degradation
- Layer 2/3/4 - forwarding and classification
- ACLs
- Load Balancing
Wire-speed classification and forwarding based 
on
- Layer 2 - 802.1Q/p, Mac, port, VLAN, interface type 
(100/1000)
- Layer 3 - IP TOS/DS, SA/DA
- Layer 4 - TCP/UDP ports
Bandwidth Management & Congestion Control
- 2048 hardware queues per blade
- Layer 2 flow control - 802.3x
- 8 priority levels per port
- VsRED

Easy configuration of a network’s users and/or elements with little to no administration effort
Consists of…
• Ease of use
• Settings automatically replicated to all switches in network
• Automatic settings of complex network functions such as QOS & security
• Single GUI and full switch access capacity through Web based managed or NMS console 
• Unified NMS platform to support data and voice

• Works out-of-the box
• 100% device management coverage
